This creates a paradox: the tasks that most need careful metacognitive regulation are the tasks for which regulatory capacity is most limited. How should this constraint be managed by learners and instructional designers?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> 1. If you were to design a two-week intervention targeting metacognitive regulation for a graduate student who consistently overestimates her comprehension of difficult texts, which failure mode would you target first — monitoring inaccuracy, regulatory poverty, or motivational interruption — and how would you diagnose which is operative?
> 2. The synthesis in this report identifies four analytical threads. Which strikes you as most underemphasized in the learning science literature you have previously encountered, and what would a research program targeting that gap look like?
> 3. This report's original contribution — the Calibration-Action Gap — is a theoretical proposal rather than an established finding. What experimental paradigm would you design to test whether the Gap is a genuinely distinct failure mode rather than a more severe form of monitoring inaccuracy?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Consider a domain where you have considerable expertise. How does your metacognitive monitoring in that domain differ from your monitoring in a domain where you are a novice? What does this suggest about the domain-generality of metacognitive skill?
> 2. Hattie's synthesis finds metacognitive strategy instruction among the most effective educational interventions, yet it remains underused in most schools. What barriers — pedagogical, institutional, or cultural — might account for this gap between evidence and practice?
> 3. The Dunning-Kruger paradox holds that those lacking competence also lack the metacognitive capacity to diagnose that lack. What systems, external prompts, or social structures can compensate for this self-concealing limitation?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. How would you distinguish, in practice, between a learner who lacks metacognitive knowledge (does not know good strategies exist) and a learner who lacks metacognitive regulation (knows strategies but fails to deploy them)? What different interventions would each require?
> 2. The fluency illusion is one of the most robust findings in metacognition research, yet it is rarely taught explicitly to students. What instructional approaches might cultivate resistance to the fluency illusion before it becomes entrenched?
> 3. Flavell distinguishes metacognitive knowledge (stable, long-term) from metacognitive experience (transient, online). Can you identify a specific metacognitive experience you had recently — and trace how, or whether, it updated your metacognitive knowledge?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Nelson and Narens distinguish monitoring from control as separate processes. Can you identify a situation in which your monitoring was accurate (you correctly detected a comprehension gap) but your control response was absent or insufficient? What would have needed to be different for the control response to follow?
> 2. The finding that delayed JOLs are more accurate than immediate JOLs has a direct implication for how one should study. How does your current study practice align with or deviate from this finding? What changes would bring your practice into better alignment?
> 3. The discrepancy reduction model suggests learners tend to avoid the hardest material — focusing on intermediate-difficulty items. Do you recognize this bias in your own learning? If so, what strategies might systematically override it?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Dunlosky's meta-analysis found that students overrate rereading and highlighting while underrating distributed practice and retrieval practice. Do your own study preferences diverge from the empirical evidence? If so, what maintains the preference for ineffective strategies in light of evidence to the contrary?
> 2. The "entity theory vs. incremental theory" distinction Dweck identifies is a specific instance of person knowledge. In what domains do you hold entity-theory beliefs ("I'm either good at this or not") versus incremental-theory beliefs ("this is learnable with effort")? What produced this asymmetry?
> 3. Conditional metacognitive knowledge is the most difficult to develop. Identify a specific study strategy you use. Can you articulate the conditions under which it is optimal, suboptimal, and should be replaced by a different approach? If not, what does this suggest about the development of your conditional knowledge?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Zimmerman's self-reflection phase involves causal attribution of performance gaps. When you recently performed worse than expected on a task, what attribution did you make? Was it constructive (modifiable cause) or fixed (stable cause)? How did it influence your subsequent behavior?
> 2. Reciprocal teaching trains four specific strategies: predicting, questioning, clarifying, and summarizing. The last time you read a complex text, how systematically did you employ these regulatory processes? What would applying all four have added?
> 3. The paradox of automaticity suggests that experts are vulnerable to specific types of metacognitive oversight failures in their domain of expertise. Can you identify a domain where your expertise creates blind spots — places where you are so fluent you no longer monitor?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Ann Brown's metacognitive inertia describes strategies that exist in the repertoire but don't get deployed automatically. Can you identify strategic knowledge you possess — including knowledge from this report — that you have not consistently applied in your own learning practice? What maintains the inertia?
> 2. The expert-novice monitoring divide suggests that you can only monitor what your knowledge schemas make visible. In a domain where you are currently a beginner, what aspects of your own performance are you probably not yet able to monitor because you lack the knowledge frameworks that would make them visible?
> 3. The Dunning-Kruger pattern describes metacognitive overconfidence at low expertise, increased uncertainty at intermediate expertise, and calibrated confidence at high expertise. Where in this pattern do you fall in your primary domain of expertise? What evidence would confirm or disconfirm your self-assessment?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. The distinction between training the what/how of a strategy versus the what/how/when/why suggests two very different instructional designs. Reflect on skill-training you have received — in your education, professional development, or personal learning — and evaluate: were you given the rationale that makes transfer possible, or only the mechanics?
> 2. Desirable difficulties feel counterproductive during learning. Identify one desirable difficulty (retrieval practice, interleaving, spacing) you systematically avoid in favor of a more comfortable alternative. What would committed adoption of that difficulty look and feel like?
> 3. Growth mindset research shows that how learners interpret difficulty signals — threat or challenge — creates divergent developmental trajectories. What language do you currently use when you make metacognitive monitoring observations about your own performance? Is that language entity-framing or incremental-framing?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. The illusion of knowing predicts that you have mistaken processing fluency for comprehension depth in your own recent learning. Can you identify material you studied recently and found fluent that you now suspect you understood shallowly? What would testing yourself on that material now reveal?
> 2. Hindsight bias predicts that you remember your prior predictions as being more accurate than they were. Identify an important decision or prediction you have recently made. Can you accurately reconstruct the uncertainty distribution you held before the outcome was known?
> 3. The deepest metacognitive challenge is knowing that your monitoring has unknown blind spots. What practices do you currently have — testing, expert feedback, peer review — that implement external monitoring standards capable of revealing what your internal monitoring cannot access?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If the meta-level's model of the object-level is always constructed from cues rather than from direct access, what determines which cues are available and which are ignored — and how might cue availability differ across domains of expertise?
> 2. Does the two-level architecture imply that metacognition is fundamentally a single mechanism applied across all cognitive domains, or might there be domain-specific monitoring systems with different informational properties?
> 3. How does the representational commitment of the Nelson–Narrens model relate to your understanding of the [[Dunning-Kruger-Effect|Dunning-Kruger effect]] — the finding that low performers tend to overestimate their competence?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. To what extent does the information-processing heritage of the Nelson–Narrens model constrain what it can explain about metacognition in emotionally charged learning situations, such as high-stakes examinations or identity-relevant performance domains?
> 2. How does the cybernetic feedback loop model of metacognitive control compare with the more elaborated cyclical models of [[Self-Regulated-Learning|self-regulated learning]] proposed by [[Barry-Zimmerman|Zimmerman]] and [[Pintrich|Pintrich]]?
> 3. If Narrens's contribution was to make the model's claims precise enough to be falsifiable, what specific predictions does the model make that competing accounts of metacognition do not?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If monitoring is always cue-mediated and never direct, what implications does this have for the design of learning environments — specifically, for the kinds of feedback that should be provided to learners to improve the diagnostic validity of their monitoring cues?
> 2. How does Koriat's three-way distinction among intrinsic, extrinsic, and mnemonic cues map onto your experience of studying? Can you identify situations in which you relied on mnemonic cues (especially fluency) that turned out to be diagnostically misleading?
> 3. Does the structural impossibility of perfect monitoring within the Nelson–Narrens framework suggest that efforts to improve metacognitive accuracy are limited in principle, or merely that they must work *with* the cue-mediated architecture rather than against it?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. Under what conditions might accurate monitoring actually *impair* control — for instance, by inducing anxiety or learned helplessness when the meta-level correctly identifies a large gap between current knowledge and the desired standard?
> 2. How does the region of proximal learning hypothesis relate to [[Vygotsky's|Vygotsky's]] concept of the [[Zone-of-Proximal-Development|zone of proximal development]]? Are the structural parallels coincidental, or do they point to a deeper principle of optimal challenge in learning?
> 3. If monitoring–control decoupling is common, what interventions might strengthen the coupling — and are such interventions more effectively targeted at improving monitoring accuracy or at improving the learner's strategic control repertoire?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. How does the cue ecology of each metacognitive judgment type relate to the types of learning activities that might improve monitoring accuracy — for instance, would retrieval practice preferentially improve the accuracy of delayed JOLs relative to immediate JOLs, and if so, why?
> 2. If FOK judgments are based on the quantity and fluency of accessible partial information rather than on the correctness of that information, what does this imply about the reliability of subjective "feeling of knowing" experiences as a guide for study decisions?
> 3. Under what conditions might retrospective confidence be *negatively* correlated with accuracy, and what cue-utilisation patterns would the Nelson–Narrens model predict in such cases?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. How might the distinction between calibration and resolution be applied to design more targeted metacognitive interventions in educational settings — for instance, interventions aimed specifically at improving threshold decisions vs. allocation decisions?
> 2. For each gradient in the Three-Gradient Monitoring Failure Taxonomy, can you identify a personal learning experience in which that type of monitoring failure occurred? Which gradient do you think is most common in your own metacognitive processing?
> 3. What are the limits of metacognitive calibration training? If some degree of monitoring inaccuracy is structural (as the Nelson–Narrens model implies), what is the realistic ceiling for calibration improvement?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If the Nelson–Narrens model is the cognitive core of SRL frameworks, what would it take to build an equally precise model of *motivational* monitoring and control — a model that specifies the informational channels through which the meta-level monitors and regulates motivation with the same rigour that Nelson and Narrens bring to cognitive monitoring?
> 2. Does the embeddedness of the Nelson–Narrens model within SRL frameworks enhance or constrain the model's explanatory power? Could the monitoring–control architecture be productively applied in domains outside education — for instance, in clinical psychology, organisational behaviour, or artificial intelligence?
> 3. To what extent does the gap between self-report measures of metacognition (like the MAI) and actual metacognitive performance undermine the construct validity of metacognitive assessment, and how might more behaviourally anchored measures (drawing on JOLs, FOKs, and calibration data) improve the situation?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If the Nelson–Narrens architecture is a specific instance of a general hierarchical predictive processing principle, what would this imply for the ontological status of metacognition — is it a distinct psychological capacity, or merely one manifestation of a ubiquitous computational motif?
> 2. Which of the unresolved tensions (level-number, affect-cognition, social dimension, computational specification) do you think is most consequential for the future development of the model, and why?
> 3. How might the Nelson–Narrens model need to be revised to accommodate findings from [[cognitive-neuroscience|cognitive neuroscience]] that suggest metacognitive monitoring may involve distinct neural circuits rather than a single, unified meta-level?
































































































## Spaced Repetition Seeds

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the difference between a textbase and a situation model in reading comprehension?
> **A:** **A:** A *textbase* is a propositional semantic representation of what the text explicitly says — a network of propositions derived from the text's surface content. A *situation model* is a richer mental model of the actual situation, event, or concept described by the text, incorporating prior knowledge, inferences, spatial relationships, and causal structure. Situation models support transfer and problem-solving; textbase representations support paraphrase and recognition.
> **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #reading-comprehension #cognitive-science

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Fluency Illusion, and why does it undermine metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** **A:** The Fluency Illusion is the systematic tendency to interpret *ease of text processing* (perceptual fluency) as evidence of *comprehension depth*, when fluency and comprehension are not reliably related. Re-reading increases fluency without necessarily improving situation model quality — but the increased fluency feels like improved understanding. This creates false metacognitive confidence and is a primary driver of the passive re-reading trap.
> **Source:** Section 8.2 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What are the three sub-types of metacognitive knowledge, and which is most predictive of strategic reading proficiency?
> **A:** **A:** (1) *Declarative* — knowing *what* strategies exist; (2) *Procedural* — knowing *how* to execute strategies; (3) *Conditional* — knowing *when* and *why* to use which strategy. Conditional metacognitive knowledge is most predictive of advanced reading proficiency and hardest to develop through instruction.
> **Source:** Section 3.1 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #metacognition #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Matthew Effect in reading, and what does this report claim about its interaction with metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** **A:** The Matthew Effect describes how early reading advantages (vocabulary, comprehension) compound over time — rich get richer, poor get poorer in literacy development. This report's synthesis argues that the Matthew Effect creates a *double failure trap*: poor domain knowledge simultaneously degrades comprehension (less effective schema application) and degrades metacognitive monitoring accuracy (schemas needed for monitoring failures are absent). Both deficits compound each other.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #matthew-effect #metacognition #prior-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** According to Dunlosky et al. (2013), which learning strategies have high utility? Which have low utility?
> **A:** **A:** **High utility:** retrieval practice (practice testing) and spaced repetition. **Moderate utility:** elaborative interrogation, self-explanation, interleaving. **Low utility:** re-reading and highlighting. The high-utility strategies are underused by most students despite being most effective; the low-utility strategies are overused because they feel effective (due to Fluency Illusion).
> **Source:** Section 5.2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #learning-strategies #retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is Reciprocal Teaching, and what four strategies does it involve?
> **A:** **A:** Reciprocal Teaching (Palincsar & Brown, 1984) is a scaffolded reading comprehension intervention involving small groups rotating through four metacognitive strategies: (1) **Summarizing** — capturing main points to check integration; (2) **Question generating** — formulating key questions to promote elaboration; (3) **Clarifying** — addressing confusing terms or passages; (4) **Predicting** — using text structure to anticipate upcoming content. Each strategy constitutes a component of the metacognitive monitoring-and-control cycle.
> **Source:** Section 7.2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #reciprocal-teaching #reading-instruction #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Zone of Proximal Development, and how does it apply to reading comprehension instruction?
> **A:** **A:** Vygotsky's ZPD defines the range of tasks a learner cannot manage independently but can manage with appropriate support. In reading instruction, the ZPD identifies texts and tasks where comprehension is possible with scaffolding but would fail without it. Optimal instruction targets the ZPD — neither too easy (no challenge) nor too difficult (incomprehensible even with support). Scaffolding is gradually reduced as the learner's independent competence develops (I-We-You progression).
> **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #vygotsky #scaffolding #zone-of-proximal-development

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is conditional metacognitive knowledge, and why is it more powerful than declarative or procedural metacognitive knowledge for reading?
> **A:** **A:** Conditional metacognitive knowledge is knowing *when* and *why* to apply specific reading strategies — understanding the match between strategy type, text type, and comprehension goal. It is more powerful than declarative knowledge (knowing strategies exist) or procedural knowledge (knowing how to execute them) because it enables strategic flexibility: deploying the right strategy in the right context rather than applying favorite strategies indiscriminately. This strategy-selection capacity is the metacognitive master skill.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #conditional-knowledge #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is elaborative interrogation, and what mechanism explains its effectiveness?
> **A:** **A:** Elaborative interrogation is a reading strategy involving self-generated "Why is this true?" questions after encountering factual or explanatory claims. Its effectiveness is explained by multiple converging mechanisms: (1) prior knowledge activation (retrieving related schemas); (2) semantic elaboration (connecting the claim to a knowledge network, creating multiple retrieval pathways); (3) inference generation (producing causal inferences beyond text information); (4) comprehension monitoring (inability to generate a plausible "why" signals comprehension failure).
> **Source:** Section 5.2 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation #deep-processing

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control model, and what is the key distinction it draws?
> **A:** **A:** The Nelson-Narens (1990) model describes metacognitive functioning as a two-level system: the *object level* (the actual cognitive reading processes — word recognition, sentence parsing, situation model construction) and the *meta level* (the monitoring and control system operating on object-level processes). *Monitoring* = information flow from object to meta level (the meta system's observation of comprehension state). *Control* = information flow from meta to object level (executive regulation of reading behavior in response to monitoring). Key insight: monitoring and control are distinct — readers can monitor failures without deploying appropriate control responses.
> **Source:** Section 3.2 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognition #nelson-narens #monitoring-control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four mediating mechanisms through which goals enhance performance according to Locke and Latham's goal-setting theory?
> **A:** Direction (focusing attention on goal-relevant activities), effort (difficult goals mobilise greater effort), persistence (goals sustain effort over time), and strategy development (goals promote the search for task-relevant strategies when existing approaches prove insufficient).
> **Source:** Section 1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #goal-setting-theory #locke-latham #mechanisms

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the critical difference between autonomous motivation and controlled motivation in SDT?
> **A:** Autonomous motivation involves volitional engagement — acting from self-endorsed interest, values, or identity. Controlled motivation involves compliance — acting from external pressure (external regulation) or internalised guilt/self-worth contingencies (introjection). Both can produce behaviour, but autonomous motivation produces deeper processing, greater persistence, and enhanced well-being.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #sdt #autonomous-motivation #controlled-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** In the 2×2 achievement goal framework, what distinguishes mastery-approach from performance-approach orientation?
> **A:** Mastery-approach defines success as developing competence and deepening understanding; performance-approach defines success as demonstrating competence relative to others. Mastery-approach is associated with deep processing and intrinsic interest; performance-approach is associated with surface processing but sometimes positive performance outcomes.
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #mastery-goal #performance-goal

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's self-regulated learning model and what occurs in each?
> **A:** Forethought (goal-setting, strategic planning, self-motivation beliefs), Performance (self-control strategies, self-observation/monitoring), Self-reflection (self-evaluation against standards, causal attribution, adaptive/defensive inferences).
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #srl #zimmerman #learning-phases

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** How does the overconfidence effect threaten self-assessment in PKM, and what mechanisms can improve calibration?
> **A:** The overconfidence effect causes learners to systematically overestimate their understanding, especially after superficial exposure. Calibration-improving mechanisms include: generation-based testing (explaining without notes), application-based assessment (using concepts in novel situations), connection-based evaluation (linking to other concepts), and temporal spacing (reassessing after delay).
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition #calibration #self-assessment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What is the specificity-autonomy dialectic and why is it the central design tension in PKM goal-setting?
> **A:** Goal-setting theory shows that specific goals enhance performance (through variance reduction and attention focusing), while SDT shows that autonomy enhances motivational quality. These pull in opposite directions: high specificity can undermine autonomy by constraining choice, while high autonomy can sacrifice the direction-giving benefits of specificity. The resolution is to be specific about *what understanding looks like* while preserving autonomy over *how that understanding is pursued*.
> **Source:** Section 2, 6, 8
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #specificity-autonomy #goal-design #motivational-grammar

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the motivational grammar framework integrate the requirements of the four theoretical frameworks?
> **A:** Template: [Understanding verb] + [domain specification] + [capability criterion] + [autonomy marker] + [temporal integration]. Understanding verb → mastery orientation (AGT). Domain specification → specificity (goal-setting theory). Capability criterion → calibration improvement (metacognition). Autonomy marker → need satisfaction (SDT). Temporal integration → planning support (SRL).
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #motivational-grammar #goal-design #integration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes formative assessment from summative assessment, and why is formative assessment the appropriate model for PKM?
> **A:** Formative assessment is designed to inform and improve ongoing learning (assessment *for* learning); summative assessment certifies or ranks attainment (assessment *of* learning). Formative assessment's three functions — making criteria transparent, providing evidence of current standing, and indicating gap-closing actions — align with PKM's purpose of continuous knowledge development rather than terminal certification.
> **Source:** Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #formative-assessment #pkm-design #self-assessment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four dimensions of linguistic microstructure in PKM goals that this report identifies as causally operative?
> **A:** (1) Verb choice — understanding verbs vs. production verbs activates mastery vs. performance orientation. (2) Object specification — understanding-focused vs. output-focused objects. (3) Autonomy markers — language preserving choice over method. (4) Temporal framing — integrated timeframes vs. controlling deadlines.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #goal-language #linguistic-framing #motivational-grammar

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does introjection function as a motivational hazard in self-directed PKM practice?
> **A:** Introjection is partially internalised regulation driven by guilt, anxiety, or self-worth contingency rather than genuine valuing. In PKM, it manifests when self-imposed assessment triggers self-punishment ("I *should* have finished this") rather than informational processing ("What does this difficulty tell me about my strategy?"). Assessment structures must be designed to function informationally (supporting competence) rather than controllingly (triggering introjection).
> **Source:** Section 2, 7
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #introjection #sdt #self-assessment #pkm-hazards

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** How did Flavell (1976) define metacognition, and what are its two major components as distinguished by Brown (1987)?
> **A:** Flavell defined metacognition as "cognition about cognition" — thinking about one's own thinking processes. Brown distinguished two components: *knowledge of cognition* (awareness of cognitive processes, strategies, and states — including declarative, procedural, and conditional knowledge) and *regulation of cognition* (executive control including planning, monitoring, and evaluating).
> **Source:** Section I
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #definition, #foundational

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between a judgment of learning (JOL) and a feeling of knowing (FOK)?
> **A:** JOLs occur *during or after study* and estimate how well material has been encoded ("How well have I learned this?"). FOKs occur *during failed retrieval* and estimate the likelihood of future recognition ("I can't recall this now, but I think I would recognise it"). JOLs assess encoding quality; FOKs assess the residual trace of information that cannot be currently retrieved.
> **Source:** Section III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-monitoring, #distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the three phases of Zimmerman's self-regulated learning cycle and their PKM workflow equivalents.
> **A:** (1) *Forethought phase* (goal-setting, strategic planning) → PKM capture and planning workflows. (2) *Performance phase* (strategy implementation, self-monitoring) → Active note-making with elaboration and connection. (3) *Self-reflection phase* (self-judgment, self-reaction) → Review, evaluation, and system modification.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning, #pkm, #process

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 4 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the fluency illusion undermine metacognitive calibration in the context of PKM practice?
> **A:** The fluency illusion causes practitioners to mistake *familiarity with material* for *understanding of material*. In PKM, re-reading one's own notes produces high processing fluency (the text is familiar), which the monitoring system misinterprets as evidence of comprehension. This inflates confidence without improving actual knowledge, worsening calibration. The antidote is retrieval-based review: attempting to recall content before reading it.
> **Source:** Sections III, V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion, #calibration, #pkm

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 5 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three tiers of the Extended Metacognitive Architecture Model (EMAM)?
> **A:** *Tier 1: Internal metacognition* — classical neural monitoring and regulation (flexible but noisy). *Tier 2: Scaffolded metacognition* — external systems that support internal processes (templates, visualisations, schedules; reduces bias but requires active engagement). *Tier 3: Extended metacognition* — external systems that *constitute* metacognitive processes through functional integration (algorithms that perform monitoring/control autonomously; most reliable but risks atrophy).
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #emam, #extended-metacognition, #framework

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 6 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the key difference between scaffolded metacognition (Tier 2) and extended metacognition (Tier 3) in the EMAM?
> **A:** In *scaffolded metacognition*, the cognitive agent remains the locus of metacognitive processing — the external system provides input and structure, but the human does the monitoring/regulation work. In *extended metacognition*, the external system *performs* the metacognitive functions autonomously through functional integration — the processing is genuinely distributed between internal and external components. The distinction turns on whether the human or the system is doing the metacognitive work.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #emam, #distinction, #extended-mind

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 7 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What is the monitoring-control coupling, and why does it matter for PKM design?
> **A:** The monitoring-control coupling (Nelson & Narens) is the degree to which monitoring outputs are effectively translated into regulatory control actions. High coupling: problem detected → corrective action taken. Low coupling: problem detected → no effective response. PKM design can tighten this coupling by embedding regulatory responses in monitoring mechanisms (e.g., spaced repetition adjusting review difficulty automatically) and make it visible through logging both monitoring events and regulatory responses.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #monitoring-control-coupling, #pkm-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 8 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** List the four phases of the Metacognitive PKM Lifecycle and the dominant metacognitive characteristic of each.
> **A:** (1) *Unconscious Capture* — metacognitive monitoring dominated by fluency illusions; regulation ad hoc. (2) *Structured Processing* — external scaffolding provides regulatory structure; monitoring improves through retrieval practice. (3) *Reflective Integration* — practitioner monitors and regulates *system use* itself; calibration improves. (4) *Metacognitive Mastery* — flexible, context-sensitive regulation; conscious management of the internal-external metacognition balance.
> **Source:** Section VII
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #lifecycle, #development, #pkm

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 9 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is cognitive offloading, and what is the "PKM calibration paradox" it creates?
> **A:** Cognitive offloading is using external resources to reduce internal cognitive demands. The PKM calibration paradox: creating comprehensive notes can *worsen* calibration because the practitioner mistakes *access fluency* (knowing where to find information in their system) for *retrieval fluency* (actually knowing the information). The notes serve as external memory that the practitioner mistakenly attributes to their internal memory, inflating confidence.
> **Source:** Section V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cognitive-offloading, #calibration, #paradox

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the parity principle from the extended mind thesis apply to metacognitive tools?
> **A:** The parity principle states: if an external process functions in a way that, were it done internally, we would count it as cognitive, then it IS cognitive regardless of physical location. Applied to metacognition: if a PKM system's spaced repetition algorithm performs the same function as an internal FOK judgment (assessing whether knowledge is sufficiently consolidated for retrieval), the parity principle suggests we should count it as a metacognitive process — making the system part of the agent's metacognitive architecture.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #parity-principle, #extended-mind, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the two primary functional components of metacognition according to Schraw and Dennison (1994)?
> **A:** Knowledge of Cognition (what one knows about cognitive processes, tasks, and strategies) and Regulation of Cognition (how one monitors, plans, evaluates, and adjusts ongoing cognitive engagement).
> **Source:** Phase II, Conceptual Foundations
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #knowledge-of-cognition, #regulation-of-cognition, #schraw

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Nelson-Narens framework distinguish monitoring from control?
> **A:** Monitoring is the causal relationship flowing from the object-level to the meta-level (information about ongoing cognitive processing flows upward). Control is the reverse: the meta-level issues commands that modify object-level processing. They are analytically distinct but empirically interleaved.
> **Source:** Phase II; Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #monitoring, #control, #nelson-narens

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes a Judgment of Learning (JOL) from a Feeling of Knowing (FOK)?
> **A:** A JOL is made after studying an item, estimating future recall probability. A FOK arises specifically after a retrieval failure, estimating whether the unrecalled item could be recognized or retrieved later. JOLs are post-study prospective; FOKs are post-retrieval-failure prospective.
> **Source:** Phase II; Appendix Section 1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #judgment-of-learning, #feeling-of-knowing, #monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Fluency Illusion and why does it impair metacognitive monitoring accuracy?
> **A:** The Fluency Illusion is the misattribution of processing fluency (ease of processing) as evidence of deep learning or comprehension. It impairs monitoring accuracy because fluency is a non-diagnostic cue for long-term retention — re-reading produces fluency without improving retention, but the learner's JOLs become inflated because the monitoring system uses fluency as its primary cue.
> **Source:** Phase IV; Appendix Section 1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #fluency-illusion, #monitoring, #calibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is calibration in the metacognitive sense, and what is the predominant direction of miscalibration in the research literature?
> **A:** Calibration is the alignment between subjective confidence and objective accuracy — a well-calibrated learner who expresses 70% confidence should achieve approximately 70% accuracy. The predominant direction of miscalibration is over-confidence: most learners, in most domains, express confidence that exceeds their actual accuracy.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #calibration, #overconfidence, #monitoring-accuracy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the delayed JOL effect, and what is the theoretical explanation?
> **A:** Delayed JOLs (made 1-2 minutes after study) are substantially more accurate predictors of subsequent recall than immediate JOLs. The explanation is cue-based: delayed JOLs allow time for an accessibility test (partial retrieval attempt), making retrieval fluency the primary cue — which is more diagnostic than the processing fluency cues available immediately after study.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #judgment-of-learning, #delayed-jol, #cue-utilization

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is Flavell's distinction between metacognitive knowledge and metacognitive experiences?
> **A:** Metacognitive knowledge is stable, explicitly accessible propositional knowledge about cognitive processes, tasks, and strategies. Metacognitive experiences are phenomenological states that arise during cognitive engagement and carry monitoring information — feelings of difficulty, confidence, familiarity, and satisfaction that signal the quality of ongoing processing in real time.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #flavell, #metacognitive-experience, #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What is Reciprocal Teaching, and why is it considered the most empirically validated metacognitive instruction method?
> **A:** Reciprocal Teaching (Palincsar & Brown, 1984) uses dialogue-based practice in four comprehension-monitoring strategies (prediction, questioning, clarification, summarization) within a ZPD framework, with a teacher gradually transferring metacognitive responsibility to students. Effect sizes consistently range from 0.4 to 0.9 on reading comprehension measures. It is most validated because it addresses both monitoring (comprehension standards) and regulation (strategy deployment) while using the Vygotskian scaffolding mechanism.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #reciprocal-teaching, #comprehension-monitoring, #instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish declarative, procedural, and conditional metacognitive knowledge.
> **A:** Declarative: propositional knowledge about cognitive processes and one's own cognitive characteristics (knowing that distributed practice outperforms massed practice). Procedural: knowledge of how to execute cognitive strategies (knowing how to implement retrieval practice). Conditional: knowledge of when and why to deploy particular strategies — the discriminative competence specifying appropriate context for each strategy. Conditional knowledge is the most neglected and most practically significant.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #conditional-knowledge, #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Dunning-Kruger effect relate to Nelson-Narens' monitoring-control architecture?
> **A:** The Dunning-Kruger effect — low-competence individuals' greater over-confidence — is a direct instantiation of monitoring failure in the Nelson-Narens framework. Low domain knowledge impairs both object-level performance and meta-level monitoring accuracy (because the knowledge required to detect performance failures is the same domain knowledge lacking in poor performers). The meta-level's model of object-level performance is thus doubly impaired.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #dunning-kruger, #calibration, #nelson-narens

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What is the calibration-regulation gap, and what variables are known to close it?
> **A:** The calibration-regulation gap is the discrepancy between accurate monitoring (detecting comprehension failures or performance gaps) and effective regulatory action (initiating corrective strategies). Accurate detection does not guarantee correction. Variables known to close the gap include: growth mindset orientation (interpreting difficulty as actionable information), mastery goal orientation (focusing on improvement rather than performance demonstration), and high self-efficacy for regulatory action.
> **Source:** Phase IV; Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #calibration, #regulation, #growth-mindset

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is pseudometacognition, and what practical risk does it pose?
> **A:** Pseudometacognition is the performance of external metacognitive behavior patterns (completing monitoring checklists, articulating study plans, answering self-questioning prompts) without genuine internal monitoring or regulatory adjustment. The practical risk is that learners taught explicit metacognitive protocols may comply with the external script without developing genuine regulatory competence — the protocol becomes a performance rather than a cognitive tool.
> **Source:** Phase V; Appendix Section 1
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #pseudometacognition, #metacognitive-instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Why does comprehension monitoring fail in novice/immature readers, according to Brown's research?
> **A:** Novice readers typically monitor only against the lexical standard (do I know the words?), missing higher-order cohesion and consistency standards. This produces the paradox of feeling comprehension has occurred (because words were decoded) when in fact no coherent situation model has been constructed. Skill development involves expanding the standards against which monitoring operates, not merely the monitoring process itself.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #comprehension-monitoring, #brown, #situation-models

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What does the Nelson-Narens framework mean by the "monitoring-first principle"?
> **A:** The monitoring-first principle is the theoretical claim that regulation can be no more accurate than the monitoring on which it is based: accurate monitoring is a necessary precondition for appropriate regulatory action. If monitoring is inaccurate (inflated confidence from fluency-based cues), even sophisticated regulatory strategies will be deployed at the wrong times or not deployed when needed. The practical implication is that metacognitive instruction must target monitoring accuracy, not merely regulatory strategy knowledge.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #monitoring, #control, #nelson-narens, #calibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 15 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Extended Mind Theory ground the claim that PKB architecture can function as external metacognitive infrastructure?
> **A:** The Extended Mind Theory (Clark & Chalmers) holds that cognitive processes can be genuinely extended into the environment through well-designed cognitive scaffolding — tools that satisfy the parity principle (functioning as they would if they occurred internally). A PKB's wiki-link structure externalizes knowledge connection monitoring; SRS scheduling externalizes JOL-based study termination decisions. These are not mere aids to cognition but genuine extensions of metacognitive monitoring and control function.
> **Source:** Phase V; Phase VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #extended-mind-theory, #pkb, #metacognitive-infrastructure

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes metacognitive reflection from metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** Metacognitive monitoring is continuous, often automatic, and online (occurring during task performance); it generates signals about current cognitive state. Metacognitive reflection is episodic, effortful, and offline; it operates on accumulated monitoring outputs to evaluate patterns, generate explanatory accounts, and revise metacognitive knowledge.
> **Source:** Section 3 — The Monitoring-Control Framework
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** metacognition, definitions, monitoring, reflection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Name and define the four principal metacognitive monitoring constructs in the Nelson-Narens framework.
> **A:** (1) Ease of Learning (EOL): pre-study prediction of learning difficulty. (2) Judgment of Learning (JOL): during/post-study prediction of future recall. (3) Feeling of Knowing (FOK): sense that unrecalled information is in memory and accessible. (4) Retrospective Confidence Judgment: post-response assessment of answer accuracy.
> **Source:** Section 3 — Monitoring Constructs
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Nelson-Narens, monitoring, calibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and why is it particularly dangerous for metacognitive reflection?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the error of interpreting ease of cognitive processing (fluency) as evidence of genuine understanding or learning. It is dangerous because it makes monitoring-based self-assessments unreliable — learners consistently generate overconfident JOLs for fluently processed material, terminate study prematurely, and correctly feel they understand material that retrieval testing reveals they cannot generate.
> **Source:** Section 5 — Failure Modes; Section 4 — Metacognitive Experience
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** fluency-illusion, calibration, distortions

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three sub-types of metacognitive knowledge, and what is the relationship between them?
> **A:** Declarative MK: factual knowledge *about* cognitive functioning (what influences performance). Procedural MK: knowledge *how* to use strategies and regulatory processes. Conditional MK: knowledge of *when and why* to deploy particular strategies for particular task types. The relationship is hierarchical-functional: declarative MK provides the knowledge base; procedural MK operationalizes it; conditional MK governs its deployment. All three are necessary for effective metacognitive reflection.
> **Source:** Section 4 — Metacognitive Knowledge
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** metacognitive-knowledge, declarative, procedural, conditional

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Zimmerman's SRL model, why is attributional framing the pivotal variable in the self-reflection phase?
> **A:** Because the same performance outcome can generate adaptive or maladaptive reflective responses depending on what it is attributed to. Attribution to controllable, internal, unstable factors (strategy, effort) generates specific revision hypotheses: what to change to improve. Attribution to stable, uncontrollable factors (fixed ability) terminates productive reflection by positing a cause that cannot be modified; furthermore, it damages self-efficacy and reduces motivation for future learning engagement.
> **Source:** Section 6 — Zimmerman and Attribution Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** SRL, Zimmerman, attribution, self-efficacy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinction between reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action, and what is the developmental relationship between them?
> **A:** Reflection-in-action: metacognitive reflection occurring *during* practice when unexpected outcomes arise, involving on-the-spot reframing and experimental response. Reflection-on-action: retrospective articulation of tacit knowing-in-action *after* a practice episode, with more deliberate processing time. Developmentally, practitioners typically develop reflection-on-action capacity first; reflection-in-action capacity emerges with greater expertise as the practitioner develops faster, more automated access to tacit knowing and its anomalies.
> **Source:** Section 7 — Professional Contexts (Schön)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** Schön, professional-practice, reflection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "Reflective Integration Cycle" proposed in this report, and what are its four stages?
> **A:** A proposed model of how metacognitive knowledge, monitoring outputs, and regulation interact during reflection: (1) Signal Collection: gathering accumulated monitoring outputs (JOLs, FOKs, difficulties) from a learning episode. (2) Pattern Recognition: identifying systematic patterns in those signals (consistent gaps, calibration errors, strategy misfires). (3) Explanatory Attribution: generating explanatory accounts for patterns (attributing to controllable, modifiable factors). (4) Metacognitive Knowledge Revision: updating declarative, procedural, and conditional MK with new insights.
> **Source:** Section 4 — Original Synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** original-synthesis, RIC, metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How does the concept of "epistemic sovereignty" synthesize the key themes of metacognitive reflection?
> **A:** Epistemic sovereignty names the capacity to conduct one's cognitive life on the basis of reasoned and calibrated self-knowledge rather than unreflected habit, external prescription, or accident of early training. It connects metacognitive reflection to self-directed learning and lifelong learning by framing reflection as the primary mechanism through which cognitive autonomy is achieved — moving from cognitively-reactive (responding to environmental demands without self-knowledge) to cognitively-self-directed (deploying well-understood cognitive strategies based on accurate metacognitive identity).
> **Source:** Section: Synthesis — Epistemic Sovereignty
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** original-synthesis, epistemic-sovereignty, lifelong-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Name three evidence-based strategies specifically designed to bypass the fluency illusion in metacognitive reflection.
> **A:** (1) Free recall testing before re-reading — forces retrieval rather than recognition, revealing actual memory traces. (2) Generation before verification — attempting to explain a concept fully before consulting source. (3) Transfer application — attempting to apply a principle to a novel problem type, forcing generative understanding often inaccessible to recognition-based assessment.
> **Source:** Section 5 — Warning callout on Fluency Illusion Bypasses
> **Difficulty:** Basic-to-Intermediate
> **Tags:** practical, calibration, retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the monitoring-regulation coupling, and what are its three common failure modes?
> **A:** Monitoring-regulation coupling is the relationship between the meta-level's acquisition of information about object-level state (monitoring) and the resulting commands that modify object-level processing (control). Three failure modes: (1) Monitoring-control decoupling: accurate monitoring without appropriate regulatory response (knowing but not acting). (2) Low-resolution monitoring: crude signals ("understand/don't understand") producing blunt regulatory responses. (3) Monitoring delay: detecting comprehension failures too late for proactive rather than reactive correction.
> **Source:** Section 3 — Monitoring-Regulation Coupling
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** monitoring, regulation, coupling, failure-modes

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the core distinction between metacognitive knowledge and metacognitive regulation?
> **A:** Metacognitive knowledge is the learner's relatively stable declarative understanding of cognitive tasks, strategies, and personal cognitive characteristics (stored in long-term memory). Metacognitive regulation is the activity of governing, monitoring, adjusting, and evaluating one's own cognitive processes in real time. Knowing that distributed practice is effective (knowledge) does not entail using it (regulation).
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #metacognitive-regulation, #metacognitive-knowledge, #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four primary components of metacognitive regulation according to Schraw, Moshman, and Pintrich?
> **A:** Planning (prospective goal-setting and strategy selection), Monitoring (concurrent tracking of comprehension and performance), Control (adjusting strategies and effort in response to monitoring signals), and Evaluating (retrospective assessment of outcomes and processes).
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-regulation, #planning, #monitoring, #control, #evaluating

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** In the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control model, what is the direction of information flow for monitoring vs. control?
> **A:** Monitoring is upward flow: information about object-level cognitive processes becomes available to the meta-level (allowing metacognitive judgments). Control is downward flow: meta-level evaluations generate commands that alter object-level processes (triggering strategy switches, pacing adjustments, effort allocation).
> **Source:** Phase II, Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #nelson-narens-model, #metacognitive-monitoring, #metacognitive-regulation, #meta-level

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why does it distort metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the systematic over-inference from subjective processing ease to genuine encoding depth and retention probability. Smooth, familiar processing generates high JOLs and comprehension confidence even when encoding is shallow. It distorts monitoring because mnemonic fluency cues — which are easily accessible — carry excessive weight in JOL construction relative to their actual validity as encoding-quality indicators.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion, #metacognitive-monitoring, #judgment-of-learning, #calibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is a Judgment of Learning (JOL), and what is its relationship to metacognitive monitoring accuracy?
> **A:** A JOL is a prospective metacognitive estimate (made during or after study) of the probability that a studied item will be retrievable on a future test. Monitoring accuracy (calibration) is measured by the correspondence between JOLs and actual performance (via gamma coefficient). Delayed JOLs (taken 24 hours after study) are consistently more accurate than immediate JOLs because they better reflect actual long-term retention rather than current fluency.
> **Source:** Phase II, Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #judgment-of-learning, #metacognitive-monitoring, #calibration, #nelson-narens-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Calibration-Action Gap, and how does it differ from the monitoring accuracy problem?
> **A:** The monitoring accuracy problem (calibration problem) is the failure of metacognitive judgments to accurately predict actual performance — monitoring is imprecise. The Calibration-Action Gap is the failure of adaptive control to ensue even when monitoring IS accurate — detection of difficulty does not produce appropriate regulatory response. Three mechanisms: regulatory poverty (no alternative strategies available), motivational interruption (difficulty interpreted as incapacity), regulatory overhead (insufficient working memory to execute corrective response).
> **Source:** Phase IV (original contribution)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #calibration-action-gap, #metacognitive-regulation, #monitoring-regulation-decoupling, #regulatory-poverty

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is regulatory poverty, and how does it differ from monitoring inaccuracy?
> **A:** Regulatory poverty is the condition in which accurate monitoring of a performance discrepancy fails to produce adaptive control because the learner lacks alternative strategies to deploy — the strategic repertoire is insufficient for corrective response. It is distinct from monitoring inaccuracy (where the detection itself is wrong) and motivational interruption (where the strategy is available but motivationally suppressed).
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #regulatory-poverty, #calibration-action-gap, #metacognitive-regulation, #metacognitive-strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the monitoring-control coupling problem, and what empirical evidence documents it?
> **A:** Monitoring-control coupling refers to the functional integration between monitoring signals and regulatory responses. Low coupling — monitoring accurately detects a problem but control does not respond — is documented in: strategy selection studies where comprehension monitoring does not produce strategy switching (Pressley et al.); calibration studies where good JOL accuracy does not predict adaptive study time allocation (Metcalfe & Finn). The Calibration-Action Gap is the theoretical account of this empirical pattern.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #monitoring-control-coupling, #monitoring-regulation-decoupling, #calibration-action-gap, #metacognitive-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** Why do most learners choose massed study over distributed practice, and what does this reveal about metacognitive regulation?
> **A:** Massed study produces higher subjective fluency and higher immediate JOLs than spaced study, even though spaced study produces superior long-term retention. Learners regulate study behavior partly on the basis of JOL signals — which are systematically distorted by fluency. This is the fluency illusion in study strategy self-regulation: learners are regulating (using monitoring to guide behavior) but their monitoring cues are invalid for long-term learning, producing the systematic preference for inferior strategies.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion, #spaced-repetition, #desirable-difficulties, #metacognitive-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does Efklides' MASRL model extend the Nelson-Narens framework?
> **A:** The Nelson-Narens model treats monitoring and control in largely cognitive-computational terms. Efklides' MASRL model foregrounds the phenomenological and affective dimension: metacognitive experiences (feeling of difficulty, feeling of knowing, confidence) are constitutive elements of the monitoring process that carry motivational valence. The same feeling of difficulty can motivate increased effort or trigger avoidance depending on goal orientation and self-efficacy, meaning that metacognitive regulation is motivationally conditioned — not merely cognitively determined.
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #masrl-model, #metacognitive-experiences, #efklides, #nelson-narens-model, #metacognitive-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is metacognitive scaffolding, and how does it differ from task-level scaffolding?
> **A:** Metacognitive scaffolding provides external support for regulatory processes (planning, monitoring, control) rather than for the target task content. A teacher asking "how do you know whether your current approach is working?" provides metacognitive scaffolding; a teacher giving hints about the solution provides task-level scaffolding. Metacognitive scaffolding is theoretically grounded in Vygotsky's ZPD: it enables regulatory performance within the learner's ZPD that could not be achieved independently, while modeling regulatory processes for eventual internalization.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-scaffolding, #scaffolding, #zone-of-proximal-development, #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the regulatory reflexivity problem?
> **A:** If metacognitive regulation is itself a cognitive process, it is subject to monitoring and control — which would require a higher-level regulatory process, and so on in an apparent regress. Practically, the regress terminates empirically (at some level, regulation operates without further oversight), but the principled location of this termination is unresolved. The problem has practical manifestations: excessive self-monitoring of regulatory processes can produce regulatory anxiety that impairs rather than aids performance.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #regulatory-reflexivity-problem, #metacognitive-regulation, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is pseudometacognition, and why does it matter for research and instruction?
> **A:** Pseudometacognition is regulatory-appearing behavior that does not involve genuine meta-level representation of one's cognitive states — a learner who re-reads automatically after a certain page count without monitoring comprehension, or who produces metacognitive vocabulary without actual monitoring. It matters for research because self-report measures cannot distinguish genuine from spurious regulation, and for instruction because behavioral change (more rereading) without genuine regulatory development does not transfer to novel contexts.
> **Source:** Phase V (Limitations)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #pseudometacognition, #metacognitive-monitoring, #metacognitive-regulation, #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does Ann Brown's production deficiency concept ground the distinction between metacognitive knowledge and metacognitive regulation?
> **A:** Production deficiency refers to the failure to spontaneously deploy strategies that the learner possesses and can use when instructed. It demonstrates that knowing a strategy (metacognitive knowledge) is insufficient for using it without explicit instruction (metacognitive regulation). Children displaying production deficiency have the strategic knowledge but lack the regulatory initiation — the self-monitoring and self-cueing — required for autonomous deployment. This is the empirical foundation for treating regulation as distinct from knowledge.
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #ann-brown, #metacognitive-knowledge, #metacognitive-regulation, #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 15 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three mechanisms of the Calibration-Action Gap, and how would you diagnose which is operative?
> **A:** (1) Regulatory poverty — accurate monitoring but no alternative strategies available; diagnosed by asking the learner to list corrective strategies when difficulty is detected. (2) Motivational interruption — strategies available but motivationally suppressed (difficulty interpreted as incapacity, avoidance response); diagnosed by observing first-order response to difficulty. (3) Regulatory overhead — strategies available and motivation adequate but cognitive load is too high to execute corrective response; diagnosed by observing whether regulatory failure is load-sensitive (more frequent under high cognitive demand). Each requires a different intervention.
> **Source:** Phase IV (original contribution)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #calibration-action-gap, #metacognitive-regulation, #regulatory-poverty, #motivational-interruption

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 1 — Definition: Metacognition
> **A:** **Q:** What is Flavell's definition of metacognition, and what two broad categories does it comprise?
> **A:** Metacognition is cognition about cognition — knowledge and processes that take one's own cognitive states and products as their objects. It comprises (a) metacognitive knowledge (stored declarative knowledge about person, task, and strategy factors affecting cognition) and (b) metacognitive experiences (real-time monitoring signals arising during cognitive engagement).
> **Source:** Section 2; Flavell (1979)
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** definition, metacognition, Flavell

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 2 — Framework: Nelson-Narens
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the two levels and two information flows in the Nelson-Narens metacognitive monitoring-control framework.
> **A:** Object level: first-order cognitive processes (studying, reasoning, retrieving). Meta level: metacognitive processes that observe and control the object level. Upward flow (monitoring): the meta level receives information about the object level's state (via JOLs, FOK, EOL). Downward flow (control): the meta level sends commands that modify object-level processes (study more, stop, seek help).
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** framework, Nelson-Narens, monitoring, control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 3 — Distinction: Monitoring vs. Regulation
> **A:** **Q:** How do metacognitive monitoring and regulation differ, and why does the monitoring-control gap matter?
> **A:** Monitoring is the metacognitive assessment of one's current cognitive state (JOL, FOK, comprehension check — generating information). Regulation is the behavioral response to that information (study more, switch strategies, seek help — executing change). The monitoring-control gap describes findings that accurate monitoring does not automatically translate into appropriate regulation — learners may detect a comprehension gap without changing their study approach, because regulation is a separate capacity that can fail independently of monitoring.
> **Source:** Sections 3, 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** monitoring, regulation, distinction, monitoring-control gap

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 4 — Mechanism: Fluency Illusion
> **A:** **Q:** Why does rereading feel more productive than retrieval practice, and why is this feeling misleading?
> **A:** Rereading familiar material is processed fluently (quickly, easily, without retrieval failure), and the monitoring system interprets this processing fluency as evidence of deep comprehension — the fluency illusion. Retrieval practice involves frequent retrieval failure, which the monitoring system correctly interprets as difficulty but incorrectly interprets as ineffective learning. In reality, the storage-retrieval paradox means the effortful encoding of retrieval practice produces more durable memory traces, while the fluent rereading of familiar material produces superficial reactivation without strengthened storage.
> **Source:** Sections 4, 7, 8
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** fluency illusion, desirable difficulties, retrieval practice, mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 5 — Process: Zimmerman's SRL Cycle
> **A:** **Q:** Name the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and identify one metacognitive process in each.
> **A:** (1) Forethought: task analysis and strategic planning before task engagement — selecting an evidence-based study strategy based on task demands and goals. (2) Performance: self-monitoring and self-instruction during task engagement — detecting comprehension failures and adjusting reading strategy in response. (3) Self-reflection: self-evaluation and causal attribution after task completion — assessing whether performance met goals and attributing gaps to modifiable (strategy, preparation) rather than fixed (ability) causes.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Zimmerman, SRL, three-phase cycle, regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
